of Anahuac. The Chichimecs and the Otomis, who abandoned the

Bk. VII. savage life, learned it from the Acolhuas and the Toltecs.

Among the paintings of the Mexicans, and of all these nations, there were many that were mere images, or portraits of their gods, their kings, their illustrious men, their animals, and their plants, with which the royal palaces of Mexico and Texcoco were filled. Others were historical, containing the accounts of their events, such as the first thirteen paintings of the Mendoza Collection, and that of the journey of the Aztecs, which is found in the work of the traveler Gemelli. Others were mythological, containing the mysteries of their religion, and of this kind is the volume that is preserved in the great library of the Institute of Bologna. Others were codices, in which their laws, their rites, their customs, and their duties or tributes were compiled, and such are all those of the aforesaid Mendoza Collection from the fourteenth to the sixty-third. Others were chronological, astronomical, or astrological, in which were represented their calendar, the positions of the stars, the aspects of the moon, the eclipses, and the forecasts of changes in the air. This sort of painting was called by them Tonalamatl. Dr. Sigüenza makes mention (*) of a painting of such forecasts, which he inserted in his Ciclografía mexicana. Father Acosta relates “that in the province of Yucatán there were certain volumes, folded in their fashion, in which the Indian sages kept the distribution of their times, the knowledge of the planets, of the animals, and of other natural things, and their antiquities, all of them extremely curious and diligently described,” which, according to the same author, perished through the indiscreet zeal of a parish priest, who, thinking them full of superstition, burned them, to the weeping of the Indians and the great displeasure of the curious Spaniards. Other paintings were topographical,

Aa2 or

(*) In his work entitled Libra astronómica, printed in Mexico.

p. 188

or chorographical, which served not only to represent the extent and Bk. VII.

the boundaries of possessions, but also the situation of places, the di‐

rection of the coasts, and the course of the rivers. Cortés says in his

first letter to Charles V that, wishing to know whether there was in

the Gulf of Mexico any safe harbor for ships, King Moctezuma pre‐

sented him with the whole coast painted on a sheet of paper, from

the port of Chalchiuhcuecan, where Veracruz now stands, as far as the

river Coatzacoalco. Bernal Díaz relates that the same Cortés made

use, on the long and difficult journey which he made to the province

of Honduras, of a map presented to him by the lords of Coatzacoalco,

on which were marked all the places and all the rivers of the coast

from Coatzacoalco as far as Huejacallan.

The Mexican empire was full of all these sorts of paintings; for the

painters were innumerable, and there was nothing that they did not

paint. Had they been preserved, nothing would be left to be desired

in the history of Mexico; but the first preachers of the Gospel, sus‐

pecting that there was superstition in such paintings, persecuted

them with fury. Of all that they could obtain in Texcoco, where the

principal school of painting was, they made so great a heap in the

market square that it looked like a mountain, and they set fire to it,

and the memory of many notable events was buried in those ashes.

The loss of so many monuments of their antiquity was inexpressibly

lamentable to the Indians, and it grieved the authors of the fire them‐

selves, after they perceived what they had done; but they strove to

remedy the harm, now by informing themselves by word of mouth

from the Indians, now by collecting, as an aid to history, the paintings

that had escaped their search; and although they collected many, they

did not collect as many as were needed; for from that time forward

the possessors of paintings became so jealous in guarding them, and

in concealing them from the Spaniards, that it is far too difficult to

obtain one from them.

The canvas on which they painted was of thread of maguei, or

of

p. 189

of the palm Icxotl (D), or of tanned skins, or even of paper. They made the paper from the leaves of a certain species of maguei, macer‐ Bk. VII. ated in the manner of hemp, and then washed, stretched, and

§ 48. smoothed. They made it also from the palm icxotl, from thin barks of Canvas and colors. other trees joined together and prepared with a certain gum, from silk, and finally from cotton, although we are ignorant of the manner of making it. I have had in my hands several sheets of this Mexican paper. In thickness it resembles European pasteboard, but it is softer and smoother, and one can write on it comfortably.

For the most part they made the paper in excessively long sheets, which they kept rolled up like the ancient parchment rolls of Europe, or else folded in the manner of our bed screens. The volume of Mexican paintings that is preserved in the library of the Institute of Bologna is a thick, badly tanned skin, made of several pieces, painted all over, and folded in this way.

The very beautiful colors which they employed both in their paintings and in their dyes they drew from the wood, the leaves, and the flowers of several plants, and from various mineral earths. They drew white from the mineral stone Chimaltizatl, which, when calcined, resembles fine gypsum, or from the Tizatlalli, which is a mineral earth that, after being made into a paste, kneaded like clay, and formed into little balls, takes on in the fire a white very like Spanish white. They made black from another mineral earth, foul-smelling, which for that reason they called Tlalijac, or from the soot of Ocotl, a certain species of very fragrant pine, collected in earthen vessels. Blue and azure they drew from the flower of the Matlalxihuitl and of the Xiuhquilitl pitzahuac, which is the indigo plant (E), although

The figures of mountains, rivers, buildings, plants, and animals, and above all those of men, which are seen in the paintings that re‐ Bk. VII. main from the ancient Mexicans, are for the most part out of proportion and deformed; which ought, as it seems to me, to be ascribed not so much to their ignorance of the proportions of objects, or to their slight skill, as to their haste in painting, to which the Spanish conquerors bear witness: so that, attending only to the faithful representation of things, they did not care about the perfection of their images, and therefore often contented themselves with outlines. § 49. Nevertheless I have seen among many ancient paintings certain por‐ Character of their

painting, and manner traits of the kings of Mexico in which, besides the singular beauty of of representing

objects. the colors, the proportions were seen to be exactly observed; yet, speaking in general, we shall not deny that the Mexican painters lacked far too much to reach perfection in drawing and in chiaroscuro.

The Mexicans made use in their paintings not only of simple images of objects, as some writers have claimed, but also of hieroglyphics and of characters. (*) They represented material things by their own figures, and, to abbreviate, and to save labor, paper, and colors, they were content to represent a part of the thing sufficient to make it known to those who understood; for just as we cannot understand the writings of others unless we first learn to read, so those Americans needed first to be instructed in the manner of representing objects in order to understand the paintings, which served them in place of writings. For things, then, that are wholly without figure, or have one that is hard to represent, they substituted certain characters, not verbal,

that with great diligence three plants of Chian, grown from seed sent from Mexico. They took root happily, and I had the pleasure of seeing them laden with flowers in September of the year 1777; but the frost, coming earlier that year than usual, killed them.

(*) Such authors are effectively refuted by Dr. Eguiara in the learned preface of the Biblioteca mexicana, and by ourselves also in our Dissertations.

p. 192

right-hand

that is, intended to form words, like our letters; but real, or immediately sig‐ Bk. VII. nificative of things, such as are the characters of the astronomers and of the algebraists. So that readers may form some idea of them, we wish to put before their eyes the numeral characters of the Mexicans, as also those of time, of heaven, of earth, of water, and of air. (H)

To represent a person they painted a man, or else a human head, and above it a figure expressing the meaning of his name, as is seen in the figures of the Mexican kings. To express a city or village they painted in the same way a figure expressive of what its name signified. To form their histories, or annals, they painted along the edge of the canvas, or paper, the figures of the years in as many little squares, and beside each little square the event or events corresponding to that year; and if, because the years whose history they wished to express were many, they could not all stand on one canvas, they continued on another. As for the order of representing the years and the events, the historian was free to begin them from any corner of the canvas he pleased; but with this rule, constantly observed in all the paintings I have seen, that if the painter began from the upper right-hand corner, he continued toward the left hand. If he began, and this was the more usual, from the upper corner at the left hand, he went on perpendicularly downward. If he painted the first year in the lower corner at the left hand, he continued toward the right, and if he began in the lower

(H) With regard to the numeral characters, it is to be noted that they painted as many points as there were units up to twenty. This number has its own character. From there this character was repeated up to twenty times twenty, that is, four hundred. The character of 400 was similarly repeated up to twenty times four hundred, that is, eight thousand. Then the character of eight thousand began to be repeated. With these three characters, and the points, they expressed any quantity whatever, at least up to twenty times eight thousand, or a hundred and sixty thousand. It is to be believed, although we do not know it, that for this number they had another character.

p. 193

right-hand corner, he went on perpendicularly upward; in such a manner that in the upper part of the canvas they never painted from the Bk. VII. left hand to the right, nor ever in the lower part from the right to the left; nor did they advance upward along the left side, nor downward along the right. Once this method of the Mexicans is known, it is easy to recognize at first sight where the beginning is, and where the end, of any historical painting.

It cannot be denied that such a way of signifying things was imperfect, confused, and equivocal; but the endeavor of those peoples to perpetuate the memory of events is worthy of praise, and so is their industry in supplying, however imperfectly, the lack of letters, which they would perhaps have discovered, considering their progress in civilization, had their empire not been so short-lived; at the least they would have considerably abbreviated and facilitated their paintings by the multiplication of characters.

Their paintings, moreover, ought not to be considered so much as an orderly and complete history as monuments and supports of tradition. The care that the fathers and teachers took to instruct their sons and their pupils in the history of the nation cannot be told sufficiently. They made them learn the harangues and speeches which they could not express with the brush; they put into verse the events of their ancestors, and taught them to sing them. This tradition cleared up the doubts and removed the equivocations that the paintings by themselves might have caused, and, seconded by such monuments, it immortalized the memory of their heroes, the examples of virtue, their mythology, and their rites, laws, and customs.

Nor did those peoples make use only of tradition, paintings, and songs to preserve the memory of events, but also of threads of various colors, variously knotted, called by the Peruvians Quipu, and by the Mexicans Nepohualtzitzin. This rare way of representing things, so much used in Peru, does not appear to have been employed in the countries of Anahuac except in the most remote centuries; for

History of Mexico, Vol. II. Bb no

p. 194

no traces of such monuments are found any longer. The Chevalier Bk. VII.

Boturini says that after the most diligent search he was barely able to

find one, in a place in Tlaxcala, whose threads were already spoiled

and almost consumed by time. If the settlers of South America ever

passed through the country of Anahuac, they could have left that art

there, which was then perhaps abandoned in favor of the use of

painting, which the Toltecs, or some other nation more ancient than

they, introduced.

After they had learned from the Spaniards the use of our letters,

several skillful Mexicans, Texcocans, and Tlaxcalans wrote their histo‐

ries, partly in Spanish and partly in elegant Mexican speech, which

writings are still preserved in some libraries of Mexico, as we have

noted elsewhere.

More fortunate than in painting were the Mexicans in sculpture,

§ 50. Sculpture. and in cast-metal and mosaic work. They expressed the images of

their heroes and of the works of nature better in stone, wood, gold,

silver, and feathers than on paper, either because the greater difficulty

of such works caused them to be undertaken with greater diligence,

or because the very high esteem in which they were held among those

peoples awakened talent and quickened industry.

Sculpture was one of the arts practiced by the ancient Toltecs.

Down to the time of the Spaniards there were preserved several stone

statues worked by the artisans of that nation, such as the idol of

Tlaloc placed on the mountain of the same name, which had so much

worship and veneration among the Chichimecs and the Acolhuas, and

the gigantic statues erected in the two celebrated temples of

Teotihuacan. The Mexicans already had sculptors when they left their

homeland Aztlan, since we know that the idol of Huitzilopochtli,

which they carried with them on their long migration, was made by

them at that time.

The common material of their statues was stone and wood. They

worked stone without iron, or steel, or any other instrument than a

chisel of flint. There was need

of

p. 195

of their incomparable composure and constancy in labor to overcome so great a difficulty, and to bear the slowness of such works; but they Bk. VII. succeeded in spite of such crude instruments. They knew how to express in their statues all the attitudes and postures of which the body is capable, they observed the proportions exactly, and they made, where it was required, the most minute and delicate carvings. Not only did they make whole statues, but they also carved on stones figures in low relief, such as those of Moctezuma II and of one of his sons, carved on a stone of the mountain of Chapultepec, mentioned and celebrated by Father Acosta. They also made statues of clay and of wood, using for these a copper chisel. The surprising number of their statues may be conjectured from that of their idols, which we mentioned in the preceding book. We have, moreover, to complain in this matter of the zeal of the first bishop of Mexico and of the first preachers of the Gospel; for, in order not to leave their neophytes any incentive to idolatry, they deprived us of many valuable monuments of Mexican sculpture. The foundations of the first church that was built in Mexico were of idols, and statues were counted by the thousands that were broken, to such a degree that, that kingdom having been the most abundant in works of this kind, nowadays scarcely a few can be found after the most diligent search. The conduct of those blessed religious was very praiseworthy, both for its motive and for its effects; but we could wish that the innocent statues of those peoples had not been subjected to the same condemnation as the superstitious simulacra, and that even of these some had been preserved in some place where they would not have been a stumbling block to the neophytes.

Cast-metal works were held by the Mexicans in far greater esteem than all the works of sculpture, both for the greater value of the ma‐

§ 51. terial and for the excellence of the workmanship itself. The marvels Cast-metal work. of such an art would not be credible if, besides the testimony of all who saw them, such rarities had not been sent to Europe in great abundance.

Bb2 The

p. 196

The works of gold and silver sent as a present to Charles V by the Bk. VII. conqueror Cortés filled the goldsmiths of Europe with amazement;

and these, according to the testimony of several writers of that time,

(*) declared them quite inimitable. The Mexican founders made, in

gold as well as in silver, the most perfect images of natural things.

They cast in metal a fish whose scales were alternately one of gold

and the next of silver; a parrot with movable head, tongue, and wings;

and a monkey with movable head and feet, and with a spindle in its

hand in the attitude of spinning. They set gems in gold and in silver,

and made most curious jewels of great value. Finally, such were these

works that even those Spanish soldiers who felt themselves torment‐

ed by the accursed hunger for gold prized in them the art more than

the material. This marvelous art, already practiced by the Toltecs,

whose invention or perfection they ascribed to the god Quetzalcoatl,

has been utterly lost through the abasement of the Indians and the

negligence of our Spaniards. I do not know that any remnant of those

so valuable works has remained; at the least it will be easier to find

one in some cabinet of Europe than in all New Spain. Without doubt

the desire to profit by their material prevailed over the curiosity to

preserve them.

The Mexicans also worked with the hammer, but little in compari‐

son with what they made by casting, and not comparably to the

works of the European goldsmiths; for they had no other instruments

for beating metals than stones. Nevertheless it is known that they

worked copper well, and that their axes and their pikes pleased the

Spaniards greatly. The Mexican casters and goldsmiths made up a re‐

spectable body. They rendered a particular worship to Xipe, their

tutelary god, and in his honor they held a great festival in the second

month with the most inhuman sacrifices.

But

p. 197

But nothing was so prized by the Mexicans as the works of mosaic

Bk. VII. which they made of the most delicate and beautiful feathers of birds. They therefore raised many species of very beautiful birds, with § 52. which that country abounds, not only in the palaces of the kings, Mosaic work. where there was, as we have already said, every sort of animal, but also in private houses, and at a certain time they plucked their feathers to use them in this kind of work, or to sell them in the market. They held in great esteem the feathers of those marvelous little birds which they call Huitzitzilin, and the Spaniards Picaflores, or flower-peckers, both for their delicacy and for the fineness and variety of their colors. In these and other very beautiful birds Nature supplied them with as many colors as Art knows how to employ, and some besides which she is not capable of imitating. For each work of mosaic several artisans were gathered, and after making the design and taking the measurements and the proportions, each one was charged with a part of the image; and he applied himself to it with such diligence and patience that he would often spend a whole day in matching a single feather, trying now one, now another, and examining it now from one side, now from another, until he found the one that completed the idea of perfection which he had proposed to himself. When the part that fell to each was finished, they gathered again to form the whole image. If some part was found to be out of order, they went back to work on it until they gave it its final perfection. They took the feathers with certain thin tweezers so as not to damage them, and attached them to the canvas with the tzauhtli, or with some other glutinous material; then they joined all the parts upon a little board, or upon a sheet of copper, and smoothed them gently until the surface of the image was left so even and so smooth that it seemed to have been made with the brush.

These are those images so celebrated by the Spaniards and by other European nations, in which the beholder does not know which he should praise more, the vividness and beauty of the natural colors, or the skill of the artisan and the ingenious arrangement of the art: “Which, says Father Acosta, are

justly

beautiful, so lively, and so cheerful that it delights marvelously. Some

they see painted with the brush that they yield not to the best

Not long ago there lived in Pátzcuaro, formerly the capital of the kingdom of Michoacán, where more than elsewhere such an art flour‐ Bk. VII. ished after the conquest, the last mosaic artisan who remained there, and with him an art so precious will already have come to an end, or will soon end, although it is now more than two centuries since they worked any longer with the perfection of the ancients. Several works of this kind are still preserved in the museums of Europe, and many in Mexico, but few, as it seems to me, of the sixteenth century, and none, so far as I know, made before the conquest. The mosaic which they made of broken shells was also very curious, an art which down to our own days has been preserved in Guatemala.

In imitation of such skilled artisans there were others who with various flowers and leaves formed on mats several most beautiful works to be used at certain festivals. After the reception of the faith of Jesus Christ they made such works for the adornment of our temples, which were sought after in rivalry by the Spanish nobility for the singular beauty of the workmanship. Nowadays there are many artisans in that kingdom who employ themselves in counterfeiting with silk the images of feathers; but their works are in no way comparable with those of the ancients.

Now in a nation so industrious in those arts which serve only cu‐

§ 53. riosity and luxury, there could not be lacking those that are necessary Domestic architecto life. Architecture, which is one of those arts that the necessity of ture of the Mexicans. the first men invented, was practiced by the inhabitants of the country of Anahuac, at least from the time of the Toltecs. The Chichimecs, their successors, the Acolhuas, and all the other nations of the kingdoms of Acolhuacan, of Mexico, and of Michoacán, of the republic of Tlaxcala, and of other provinces, except the Otomis, built houses and formed cities from time immemorial. When the Mexicans arrived in that country they found it full of great and beautiful cities. They, who before leaving their homeland were already well versed in architecture and accustomed to social life, constructed in their long migration many

edifices

p. 200

edifices in those places where they stayed for some years; whose reBk. VII.

mains still exist, as we have already said, on the banks of the river

Gila, in the Pimería, and near the city of Zacatecas. Reduced after‐

ward to the greatest misery on the little islands of Lake Texcoco, they

built humble huts of reeds and mud until, by the trade in fish, they

procured better materials. In proportion as their power and wealth

increased, their buildings kept increasing and improving; so that

when the conquerors arrived, their eyes found much to admire, and

their hands no less to destroy.

The houses of the poor were of reeds, or of unbaked bricks, or of

stone and mud, and the roof of a certain long and coarse hay, which is

very common in the countryside, particularly in the hot countries, or

of leaves of maguei laid on like tiles, which they somewhat resemble

both in thickness and in shape. One of the columns, or supports, of

such edifices was usually a tree of suitable thickness, in which, be‐

sides the pleasure they took in its foliage, they saved some labor and

expense. Such houses had for the most part only one room, where the

family lived with its animals, its hearth, and all its household goods. If

the family was not so poor, it had, in addition to two or three rooms,

an Ajauhcalli, or oratory, a Temazcalli, or bath, and a small granary.

The houses of the lords and of the well-to-do were of stone and

lime, and of two stories, with their halls and rooms well arranged, and

large courtyards: the flat roof of good timber, well worked, with its

terrace: the walls so well whitewashed, burnished, and shining that to

the first of the Spaniards who saw them from afar they appeared to

be of silver: the floor of cement was perfectly level and smooth.

Many of these houses were crowned with battlements, and had

their towers, and a garden attached, with its fishponds and its paths

laid out with symmetry. The great houses of the capital had for the

most part two doors, the principal one on the street, and

the

p. 201

the other on the canal. In their doorways they had no wooden doors, thinking, perhaps, that their dwellings were secure enough through Bk. VII. the severity of the laws against thieves; but to defend them from the view of passers-by they covered the doorways with reed mats, hanging on them some strings of potsherds, or pieces of broken crockery, or some other thing capable of rousing with its noise the attention of the servants whenever anyone lifted the mat in order to enter. No one was permitted to enter without the consent of the master. When necessity, or courtesy, or the claim of kinship did not justify the entrance of him who arrived at the door, he was heard there and promptly dismissed.

The Mexicans knew how to build arches and vaults (L), as is clear from their baths, from the remains of the royal palaces of Texcoco and of other buildings saved from the fury of the conquerors, and also from many of their paintings. Cornices and other particular ornaments of architecture were in use among them. They delighted in making ornaments of stone in the form of knots around their doors and windows, and in some edifices there was a great serpent of stone in the attitude of biting its tail above the main doorway, after winding its body about all the windows of the house. The walls of their edifices were straight and perpendicular, they using for this the plumb level, or some other equivalent instrument; for through the negligence of the historians we are ignorant of the instruments which they used for their buildings, as of several other things belonging to this and to other arts. Some believe that the Mexican masons, in building the walls, piled earth against them on one side and on the other, and that as the walls rose, the heaps of earth set against them rose likewise

to such a degree that the walls, until the building was finished, reBk. VII.

have been in use among the Mixtecs and other nations of the

Mexican kingdom, we do not believe that the Mexicans employed it,

considering the extreme brevity with which they finished their build‐

ings. Their columns were cylindrical, or square; but we do not know

that they had either bases or capitals. Nothing did they seek in them

more eagerly than to make them of a single piece, adorning them at

times with figures in low relief. The foundations of the great houses

of the capital were laid, on account of the little solidity of that

ground, upon a platform of thick cedar stakes driven into the earth,

whose example the Spaniards have imitated. The roof of such houses

was either of cedar, or of fir, or of cypress, or of pine, or of Ojametl:

the columns were of ordinary stone; but in the royal palaces they

were of marble, and some even of alabaster, which certain Spaniards

took for jasper. Before the reign of Ahuitzotl the walls of the houses

were of common stone; but the quarries of Tetzontli stone having been

discovered under that king on the shores of the Mexican lake, it be‐

gan to be used as the most suitable for the buildings of the capital; for

it is hard, light, and porous like a sponge, so that lime adheres to it

strongly. For such advantages, and for its color, which is a blood red,

it is even today prized above every other stone for buildings. The

pavements of their courtyards and of the temples were for the most

part of Tenayuca stone; but there were some made in checkerboards

of slabs of marble and of other valuable stones.

For the rest, although the Mexicans were in no way comparable

with the Europeans in taste for architecture, nevertheless the

Spaniards were struck with such admiration at seeing the royal

palaces of Mexico that Cortés, in his first letter to Charles V, not

finding words with which to describe them, speaks of them thus: “He

had, he says, speaking of Moctezuma, within this city (of Mexico)

houses for his dwelling so fine and so marvelous that I would not

believe

I

p. 203

I could ever express their excellence and grandeur: so that I shall say

Bk. VII. nothing else, except that there are none equal to them in Spain.” Such expressions are seen used both by Cortés himself in other places in his letters, and by the Anonymous Conqueror in his valuable account, and by Bernal Díaz in his most truthful history, all three eyewitnesses.

The Mexicans also constructed, for the convenience of inhabited § 54.

Aqueducts, and places, many good aqueducts. Those of the capital, for conducting causeways on the the water from Chapultepec, two miles distant, were two, made of lake. stone and cement, five feet high and two paces wide, constructed on a causeway built on purpose upon the lake, through which the water was conducted to the beginning of the city, and from there was divided by other, smaller conduits to supply several fountains, and chiefly those of the royal palaces. Although the aqueducts were two, the water flowed through only one at a time, and meanwhile they cleaned the other, in order always to have the water clear. There is still to be seen at Tetzcotzinco, once a pleasure place of the kings of Texcoco, the aqueduct by which the water was conducted to the royal gardens.

The aforesaid causeway of Chapultepec, as well as the others built on the lake and often mentioned by us, are incontestable monuments of the industry of the Mexicans; but it is shown still more plainly in the very ground of their city; for whereas other architects have nothing else to do but lay the foundations in the earth and raise the edifices, the Mexicans had in addition to make the ground itself on which to build, by joining with embankments some separate little islands. Besides this great labor, they had also that of constructing dikes and sluices in several places of the city to render their buildings more secure. But if in such works the industry of the Mexicans is discerned, in others their magnificence is displayed. Among the monuments of ancient architecture that still remain in the Mexican empire, very celebrated are the edifices of Mictlan in the Mixteca, in which there are

us of Mexican antiquity can be compared with the famous aqueduct

of Cempoala. This great work, worthy to rival the greatest of Europe,

was made toward the middle of the sixteenth century. It was directed,

without his even knowing the principles of architecture, by the apos‐

tolic Franciscan Francisco de Tembleque, and it was executed with

the greatest perfection by the people of Cempoala. Moved to pity by

the scarcity of water which his neophytes suffered, since what they

formerly gathered in ditches was consumed by the herds of the

Spaniards, that distinguished religious undertook to relieve at all

costs the need of those peoples. The water was too far away, and the

ground over which it had to be conducted was all mountainous and

broken; but everything was overcome by the active zeal of the mis‐

sionary, and by the industry and labor of the neophytes. They made,

then, an aqueduct of stone and lime more than thirty-two miles long,

(*) on account of the windings that had to be made in the mountains.

The greatest difficulty consisted in surmounting three great ravines

lying in the way; these too were overcome with three bridges, the first

of forty-seven arches, the second of thirteen, and the third, which is

the largest and the most surprising, of sixty-seven arches. The great‐

est arch, which is the one in the middle, situated at the greatest

depth of the ravine, is 110 geometric feet high and 61 wide, so that a

great ship could pass under it. The other sixty-six arches, situated on

one side and on the other of that greatest one, go diminishing on

each side down to the edge, or highest part

The description of this superb structure did not seem to me out of place in my History; for although it was undertaken by a Spaniard after the Conquest, it was nevertheless carried out by the people of Cempoala, who survived the ruin of their empire.

The ignorant Researcher denies to the Mexicans the knowledge and the use of lime; but it is evident from the testimony of all the historians of Mexico, from the register of tributes, and above all from the ancient edifices still standing, that all those nations made the same use of lime as the Europeans do. The common people of that kingdom believe that the Mexicans mixed eggs with the lime to make it more tenacious; but this is an error, caused perhaps by seeing the ancient walls yellowish. It is likewise evident from the testimony of the first historians that baked bricks were in use among the Mexicans, and that they were sold, like all other things, in the markets.

The stonecutters, who cut and worked the stone for edifices, did not § 56. use picks, nor chisels of iron, but only certain instruments of flint; nev‐ Stonecutters, jewel‐

ers, and potters. ertheless they made fine works and carvings in the stone. But more than such works done without iron, one wonders at the stones of stupendous size and weight that were found in the capital and elsewhere, carried from afar and placed in lofty positions without the aid of the machines which Mechanics has invented. Besides common stone, they also worked marble, jasper, alabaster, Itztli, and other valuable stones. Of Itztli they made fine mirrors mounted in gold, and those very sharp razors which they used in their swords, and of which their barbers also made use. They made such razors with such speed that a single artisan produced more than a hundred in an hour. (M)

The

(M) Dr. Hernández, Torquemada, and Vetancurt indicate the method which those artisans had of making razors from the Itztli.

p. 206

The Mexican jewelers not only had knowledge of gems, but also knew how

Bk. VII. to polish them, to work them, and to engrave them, forming on them as many figures as they wished. The historians affirm that these works were done with a certain sand; but it is certain that they could not have been done without some instrument of flint or of hard copper, such as exists in that country. The gems most in use among the Mexicans were emeralds, amethysts, carnelians, turquoises, and others unknown in Europe. Emeralds were so common that there was no lord who did not have some, and none of them died from whose corpse they did not hang an emerald at the lip, so that it might serve him, as they said, in place of a heart. Those that were sent to the court of Spain in the first years after the conquest were likewise beyond counting. When Cortés returned to Spain for the first time, he brought with him, among other inestimable jewels, five emeralds, which, as Gómara, who was then living, testifies, were valued at a hundred thousand ducats, and for one of them certain Genoese merchants wished to give forty thousand in order to resell it to the Grand Turk; (N) and besides these, two vessels, likewise of emerald, valued, according to the celebrated Father Mariana, (*) at three hundred thousand ducats, which vessels Cortés lost in the shipwreck that he suffered in the unfortunate expedition of Charles V against Algiers. Today such gems are no longer worked, nor is the place known of the mines from which they

(N) As for Cortés’s emeralds, the first was made in the form of a rose, the second like a little horn, the third like a fish with eyes of gold, the fourth was a little bell with a fine pearl for a clapper, and on it this Spanish inscription in gold letters, Bendito quien te crió, that is, Blessed be he who created you. The fifth, which was the most valuable, and for which the Genoese would have given 40,000 ducats, was a small cup with a foot of gold, and with four little chains, also of gold, which were joined together in a pearl in the manner of a button. The rim of the cup was encircled by a ring of gold, on which was engraved this Latin inscription: Among those born of women there hath not risen a greater. These five emeralds, worked by the Mexicans by order of Cortés, were presented by him to his second wife, the noble lady Doña Juana Ramírez de Arellano y Zúñiga, daughter of the Count of Aguilar: Jewels, says Gómara, who saw them, better than any lady in Spain ever had.

Bk. VII. such as a holy stone that is in the cathedral church of Puebla, and another in the parish church of Quechula (if it is not the same one transported from there to Puebla), which the parishioners kept bound with iron chains, as Vetancurt says, so that no one might carry it away.

The potters made of clay not only the vessels necessary for the use of houses, but also other works of mere curiosity, which they adorned with various colors; but they did not know, as it appears, the art of glazing them. The most famous potters were formerly the Cholulans, whose vessels were much § 57. prized by the Spaniards; today the potters of Cuauhtitlan are celebrated. Carpenters, weavers,

The carpenters worked several kinds of wood well with their copper tools, etc. some of which are to be seen even today.

The manufacture of various kinds of cloth was common everywhere, and this was one of the arts that nearly everyone learned. They had neither wool, nor common silk, nor flax, nor hemp; but they supplied the place of wool with cotton, of silk with feathers and with the hair of the rabbit and the hare, and of flax and hemp with the Icxotl, or mountain palm, with the Quetzalichtli, with the Pati, and with other species of maguei. Of cotton they made both coarse cloths and cloths as fine and delicate as those of Holland, which were rightly prized in Europe. A few years after the Conquest a priestly vestment of the Mexicans was carried to Rome, which, as the Chevalier Boturini affirms, made that court marvel at its fineness and excellence. They wove these cloths with several figures and colors, representing on them various animals and flowers. Of feathers woven in with cotton they made mantles, bedcovers, tapestries, coats, and other things no less soft than beautiful. I have seen some handsome mantles of this kind, which some lords still preserve, and are accustomed to wear on certain extraordinary festivals, such as that of the coronation of the King of Spain. In like manner they wove with cotton the finest hair

of

p. 208

of the belly of rabbits and hares, after dyeing and spinning it, and

Bk. VII.

made from it very soft cloths, and with these winter doublets for the

lords. From the leaves of the Pati and of the Quetzalichtli (kinds of

maguei) they drew a fine thread for making cloths equivalent to those

of linen, and from the leaves of other species of maguei, as from those

of the mountain palm, they drew a coarser thread, similar to hemp.

The manner they had of preparing these materials was the same that

Europeans have for flax and hemp. They macerated the leaves in wa‐

ter, and then cleaned them, put them in the sun, and beat them, until

they put them in a condition to be spun.

Of the same leaves of the mountain palm, as also of those of the

Izhuatl (another species of palm), they made very fine mats of various

colors. Others, coarser, they made of the rush that grows in abun‐

dance in that lake.

Of the thread of the maguei they also made ropes, and shoes, and

other articles.

They tanned very well the skins of animals, both those of

quadrupeds and those of birds, leaving on some the hair or the feath‐

er, and removing it from others, according to the use they wished to

make of them.

could not be equaled by our goldsmiths. This present, which was part of

what King Moctezuma made to Cortés a few days after he had landed

at Chalchiuhcuecan, was sent by that Conqueror to Charles V in July

of the year 1519; and this was the first gold and the first silver that

New Spain sent to the old: a small sample of the immense treasures

that it was to send in the future.

Among the other arts practiced by the Mexicans, medicine was en‐

tirely passed over by the Spanish historians, although it is a substan‐

tial part of their history. Those historians contented themselves with

saying that the Mexican physicians had great knowledge of herbs, and

that they performed marvelous cures with them, but without indicat‐

ing the progress they made in an art

so

p. 211

so profitable to mankind. But it cannot be doubted that the same

Bk. VII. needs which drove the Greeks to make a collection of experiments and observations on the nature of diseases and on the virtue of remedies likewise led the Mexicans to the knowledge of these two most § 59.

Knowledge of nature, important parts of medicine. and use of medicinal

We do not know that they made use of their paintings, as the simples. Greeks did of their writings, to communicate their knowledge to posterity. Those who professed medicine instructed their children in the nature and the diversity of the diseases to which the human body is subject, and in the herbs which Divine Providence has created for their remedy, the virtues of which had already been tried by their elders. They taught them the manner of discerning the different stages of diseases, of preparing the medicines, and of applying them. Of all this we have good proofs in the Natural History of Mexico written by Dr. Hernández. (P) This learned and laborious writer always had the Mexican physicians for his guides in the researches into Nature which he made in that vast empire. They made known to him twelve hundred plants with their proper Mexican names, two hundred and more species of birds, and a

Dd2 great

(P) Dr. Hernández, being physician to Philip II, King of Spain, and very renowned for his works published on the Natural History of Pliny, was sent by that monarch to Mexico to make researches into the natural things of that kingdom. He was employed there, together with other learned naturalists, for several years, availing himself of the instruction of the Mexican physicians. His work, worthy of the 60,000 ducats spent on it, consisted of 24 books of history and eleven volumes of excellent pictures of plants and animals; but the King, thinking it too voluminous, ordered his physician Nardo Antonio Recchi, a Neapolitan, to make a compendium of it. This compendium was published in the Spanish language at Mexico by Francisco Ximénez, a Dominican, in 1615, and then in Latin at Rome in 1651 by the Lincean Academicians, with learned notes and dissertations, but too long and tedious. Hernández's manuscripts were kept in the library of the Escorial, whence Father Nieremberg took a great part of what he wrote in his Natural History, as he himself avows. Father Claude Clement, a French Jesuit, speaking of Hernández's manuscripts, says thus: If all these books and commentaries were as perfect and finished as they were begun, Philip II and Francisco Hernández would in no way yield to Alexander and Aristotle in this respect.

p. 212

great number of quadrupeds, reptiles, fishes, insects, and minerals. Bk. VII.

From this most valuable, though imperfect, history there could be

formed a body of practical medicine for that kingdom, as indeed Dr.

Farfán did in his book of Cures, and so did the admirable anchorite

Gregorio López and other celebrated physicians. And if from that

time on the study of natural history had not been neglected, nor the

prejudice in favor of everything from overseas been so great, the in‐

habitants of New Spain would have been spared a great part of the

expense incurred in acquiring the drugs of Europe and of Asia, and

would have drawn greater advantage from the products of their own

country. To the Mexican physicians Europe owes tobacco, American

balsam, gum copal, liquidambar, sarsaparilla, tacamahaca, jalap, barley,

and purging nuts, and other simples which have been of great use in

medicine; but infinite are those of which Europe is deprived through

the ignorance or the negligence of merchants.

Among the purgatives used by the Mexican physicians, besides

jalap, the purging nuts, and the small bean, there was very common

among them the Mechoacan, so well known in Europe, (Q) as well as

the Izticpatli, so celebrated by Dr. Hernández, and the Amamaxtla,

commonly called friars’ rhubarb.

Among several emetics they used the Mexochitl and the

Neixcotlapatli, and among the diuretics the Axixpatli and the

Axixtlacotl, which is highly praised by the aforesaid Hernández.

Among their antidotes the famous contrahierba was justly prized,

called by them, from its figure, Coanenepilli (serpent’s tongue), and

from its effects Coapatli, that is, remedy against serpents. Among the

errhines there was the Zozojatic, a plant so efficacious that it is

enough to bring its root near the nose

To preserve themselves from the ailment which excessive exercise in the ball game used to cause them, they were accustomed to eat the bark of the Apitzalpatli macerated in water. We should never be done if we wished to mention all the plants, resins, minerals, and other medicaments, both simple and compound, which they used against all the kinds of diseases known to them. Whoever may wish fuller instruction on this subject can consult the aforesaid work of Dr. Hernández, and the two treatises published by Dr. Monardes, a physician of Seville, on the medicinal things which are usually brought from America to Europe. § 60.

The Mexican physicians made use of infusions, decoctions, plas‐ Oils, ointments, infu‐

sions, etc. ters, ointments, and oils, and all these things were sold in the market, as Cortés and Bernal Díaz, eyewitnesses, attest. The oils most in use among them were those of ule, or elastic resin, of Tlapatl, a tree resembling the fig, of Chilli, that is, chili pepper, of Chian, and of Ocotl, a species of pine. This last they drew by distillation, and the others by decoction. That of Chian served painters more than physicians.

They also drew from the Huitziloxitl, as we have mentioned elsewhere, those two sorts of balsam mentioned by Pliny and other ancient naturalists, namely the opobalsam, or balsam distilled from the tree, and the xylobalsam, obtained by the decoction of the branches. From the bark of the Huaconex, macerated four days continuously in water, they drew another liquor equivalent to balsam. From the plant called by the Spaniards Maripenda (a name, as it appears, taken from the Tarascan language), they likewise drew a liquor resembling balsam, both in its good odor and in its marvelous effects, by putting the tender stalks with the fruit of the plant to boil in water until the water became so thick

as

as must. In this manner they drew other valuable oils and liquors,

such as that of liquidambar and that of the fir.

Bloodletting was very common among the Mexicans and the other

Bloodletting and nations of Anahuac, and their physicians performed it with skill and

baths.

safety with lancets of itztli. The country people were accustomed to

draw blood from themselves, as they still do, with maguei thorns,

without availing themselves of any other hand, or interrupting the

work in which they are engaged. They also used, in place of leeches,

the spines of the Huitztlacuatzin, or Mexican porcupine, which are

thick and have a small hole at the point.

Among the means which they employed for the preservation of

their health, that of baths was much used. They bathed very fre‐

quently, and many of them daily, in the natural water of rivers, lakes,

ditches, and ponds. Experience has shown the Spaniards the useful‐

ness of such baths in that climate, and especially in the hot countries.

§ 62. The Temazcalli, or Almost as frequent among them, and among the other nations of Mexican hypocaust.

Anahuac, was the bath of the Temazcalli, which, being in all its cir‐

cumstances worthy of particular mention in the history of Mexico,

there has not been even one among the historians of that kingdom

who has described it, while they often dwell on descriptions and ac‐

counts of little importance: so that if that bath had not been pre‐

served to this day, its memory would have perished altogether.

The Temazcalli, or Mexican hypocaust, is built for the most part of

unbaked bricks. Its form is very like that of the ovens for baking

bread; but with this difference, that the floor of the Temazcalli is a lit‐

tle convex, and lower than the surface of the ground, whereas that of

our ovens is flat, and raised for the greater convenience of the bakers.

Its greatest diameter is about eight feet, and its greatest height six.

Its door, which is also like the mouth of an oven, is wide enough for a

man to enter comfortably on all fours. In the part opposite the door

there is a

furnace

PDF 228

The Temazcalli, or Mexican hypocaust.

1 The front. 2 The back. 3 Section.

p. 215

furnace of stone or of bricks, with its mouth facing outward for putting the fire in, and with a hole above for letting out the smoke. Bk. VII. The part where the furnace is joined to the hypocaust, which measures about two and a half feet square, is closed with a dry stone of tetzontli, or of another as porous as that. In the upper part of the vault there is another hole, or vent, like that of the furnace. This is the common structure of the Temazcalli, as shown in the accompanying figure; but there are others which have neither vault nor furnace, and are reduced to small oblong chambers, otherwise well covered and protected from the air.

When someone wishes to bathe, he has put inside the temazcalli first a mat, (*) a basin of water, and a bunch of herbs or of maize leaves. Then a fire is made in the furnace, and kept burning until the stones that lie between the temazcalli and the furnace are quite red-hot. He who wishes to bathe enters, for the most part naked, and often, either because of illness or for his greater convenience, is accompanied by one of his servants. As soon as he enters, he shuts the door tightly, leaving open for a little while the vent which is in the roof of the temazcalli, so that the smoke introduced through the cracks of the stones may go out; and after it has all gone out, he stops up the vent also. Then he throws water on the heated stones, from which a dense vapor at once rises, and goes to occupy the upper part of the Temazcalli. Meanwhile, as the sick man lies on the mat, his servant (if he cannot do it for himself) begins to draw the vapor down, and to beat the sick man gently, especially on the aching part, with the bunch of herbs somewhat dipped in the now warm water of the basin. The sick man at once falls into a gentle and copious sweat, which is increased as one wishes, or diminished in proportion to need. Once the desired evacuation is obtained, freedom is given

to

(*) The Spaniards, when they bathe, usually put down a mattress, for greater comfort.

p. 216

to the vapor, the door is opened, and the sick man dresses; and, well Bk. VII. covered, is carried on the mat or mattress back to the room; for the

door of the bath is for the most part inside some room of the

dwelling.

The Temazcalli has always been used in several diseases, especially

in fevers caused by some obstruction. The Indian women commonly

use it after childbirth, and also those who have been bitten or wound‐

ed by some venomous animal. It is without doubt an effective remedy

for all who need to evacuate thick and tenacious humors, and I do

not doubt that it would be most useful in Italy, where rheumatisms

are so frequent and so severe. When a more copious sweat is desired,

they lift the sick man and bring him near the vapor; for he sweats the

more, the nearer he approaches it. So common is the Temazcalli even

now that there is no place inhabited by Indians where there are not

many of them.

§ 63.

As for the surgery of the Mexicans, the Spanish Conquerors themSurgery. selves attest from their own experience to their promptness and suc‐

cess in curing wounds. (*) Besides balsam and maripenda, they used

the milky sap of the Itzontecpatli (a species of spurge), tobacco, and

other herbs. For ulcers they made use of the Nanahuapatli, the

Zacatlepatli, and the Itzcuinpatli; for abscesses and several tumors, the

Tlalamatl and the milky sap of the Chilpatli; and for the fracture of

bones, the Nacazol, or Toloatzin. After drying and reducing to powder

the seed of this plant, they mixed it with a certain resin and applied it

to the aching part, covered the part with feathers, and put little

boards over it to set the bone.

The physicians were for the most part those who prepared and ap‐

plied the medicaments; but they accompanied their cures, to render

them mysterious and more highly valued, with several

superstitious

p. 217

superstitious ceremonies; with the invocation of their gods, and with certain imprecations against diseases. The physicians venerated the Bk. VII. goddess Tzapotlatenan as the protectress of their art, and believed her to be the inventor of several medicinal secrets, and among others of the oil which they drew by distillation from the Ocotl. § 64.

It is a wonder that the Mexicans, and especially the poor, were not Foods of the

Mexicans. subject to many diseases, considering the quality of their foods. This is an article in which they had some singular things; for having been reduced, for so many years after the founding of Mexico, to the most wretched life on the little islands of the lake, they were compelled by their necessity to feed on whatever they could find in those waters. In a time so calamitous they learned to eat not only the roots of marsh plants and the aquatic serpents which abound there, the Axolotl, the Atetepiz, the Atopinan, and other such little animals of the water, but also ants, marsh flies, and the eggs of those same flies. Of these flies, which they called Axajacatl, they caught so great a quantity that they had enough to eat, to feed several species of birds, and to sell in the market. They pounded them, and made little balls of that mass, which, wrapped in maize leaves, they put to cook in water with niter. Such a dish did not displease some historians, who tasted it. From the eggs which these flies lay in the greatest abundance on the rushes of the lake they drew, as we have mentioned, that singular kind of caviar which was called by them Ahuauhtli.

Not content with feeding on living things, they also ate a certain slimy substance which floats on the waters of the lake, which they dried in the sun and preserved for use, like cheese, which it resembles in taste. They gave this substance the name of Tecuitlatl, that is, excrement of stones. Accustomed then to such vile foods, they did not give them up in the time of their greatest abundance: so that the market was always seen full of a thousand species of little animals, raw, boiled, fried, and roasted, which were sold chiefly

History of Mexico, Vol. II. Ee for

p. 218

for the poor. Nevertheless, as soon as, with the trade in fish, they began to Bk. VII.

procure better foods for themselves, and to cultivate by their industry the

floating islets in the lake, they already fared better, and at their dinners there

was nothing to be desired, neither the abundance, nor the variety, nor the

good taste of the dishes, as the Conquerors attest. (R)

Among their edibles the first place belongs to maize, which they called

Tlaolli, a grain granted by Providence to that part of the world in place of the

wheat of Europe, the rice of Asia, and the millet of Africa, with some advan‐

tages over them; for besides being wholesome and tasty, it is more nutritious,

multiplies more, grows equally well in warm climates and in cold, does not re‐

quire so much cultivation, is not so delicate as wheat, nor needs, like rice, a

damp soil harmful to the health of the cultivators. They had many kinds of

maize differing in size, in color, and in quality. Of maize they made their

bread, altogether different from the bread of Europe, no less in taste and in

shape than in the manner of making it, which they had anciently, and pre‐

serve to this day. They put the grain to cook in water with a little lime. When

it is somewhat soft, they rub it between the hands to take off the skin. Then

they grind it on the Metlatl, (*) take a little of that paste, and, spreading it

with reciprocal clapping of the hands, form the bread, and then give it its fi‐

nal cooking on the Comalli. The shape of such loaves is round and flat, their

diameter is about eight fingers, and their thickness more than a line; but they

also make them smaller and thinner, and for the lords they made them as thin

as our thickest paper. They were accustomed to mix some other thing into

the maize, to render the bread more wholesome or more delicious. For per‐

sons of rank or of means they were accustomed to

make

Method of making bread. 1. Woman husking the maize. 2. grinding it. 3. forming and baking the bread.

p. 219

make bread of red maize, kneading it with the most beautiful flower coatzontecoxochitl, and with several medicinal herbs, to lessen the heat Bk. VII. of the stomach. This is the bread that the Mexicans, and all the other nations of those vast countries, have always used down to our own days, rejecting in comparison with it the best wheat bread. Their example has been imitated by very many Spaniards; but, to tell the truth, this bread, although it is very wholesome and substantial, and when freshly made has a good taste, is nevertheless somewhat unpleasant when it grows cold. The making of bread, like the preparing and seasoning of every sort of food, has always been among those nations a trade proper to women. They were the ones who made it for their families, and the ones who sold it in the market.

Not only bread, but also very many foods and drinks they made of maize, with various ingredients and preparations. The Atolli is a gruel, or polenta, made of the paste of cooked maize, well ground, diluted in water, and strained. They put that liquid, strained through the sieve, on the fire, and give it a fresh cooking until it takes on the proper thickness. It is tasteless to the palate of the Spaniards; yet they commonly give it to their sick, as a wholesome nourishment, sweetening it with a little sugar in place of the honey formerly used by the Indians. To these, however, it is so agreeable that they cannot do without it. It was formerly, and is even today, their breakfast, and with it they bear the labors of agriculture and of the other servile occupations in which they are engaged. Dr. Hernández distinguishes seventeen species of Atolli, differing as much in the seasoning as in the manner of preparing it.

After maize, the seeds most used were those of cacao, of chia, and of kidney beans. Of cacao they made several usual drinks, and among others that called by them Chocolatl. They ground an equal quantity of cacao and of seeds of Pochotl, put it all together with a proportionate quantity of water in a small pot, and there turned it

Ee2 and

p. 220

and stirred with that small toothed instrument of wood, which in Bk. VII.

some places in Italy is called frullo, and in others macinella: then they

removed the oiliest and floating part, and put it in another vessel. For

the rest, they added a handful of paste of cooked maize, and cooked

it on the fire up to a certain point, and then, having taken it off, they

mixed in the oily part, and waited until it grew lukewarm before tak‐

ing it. This is the origin of the famous chocolate, which the civilized

nations of Europe have adopted, together with the name and the in‐

struments for making it, though altering the name somewhat, and im‐

proving the drink according to the language and the taste of each na‐

tion. The Mexicans were accustomed to put into their chocolate, and

into the other drinks which they made of cacao, either to make them

tastier or to render them more wholesome, the Tlilxochitl, or vanilla,

the flower of the Xochinacaztli, (S) and the fruit of the Mecaxochitl, (T)

and sometimes they also added honey to sweeten it, as we add sugar.

From the seed of the chia they made a very refreshing drink, very

common even today in that kingdom; and from the same seed and

from maize they made the Chianzotzolatolli, which was an excellent

drink much used by the ancients, especially in time of war. The sol‐

dier who carried with him a little bag of maize flour and chia consid‐

ered himself sufficiently provided. When there was need, he cooked

in water the quantity he wished, adding a little maguei honey; and

with such a delicious and nutritious drink (as Dr. Hernández

calls it) he endured the burning heat of the Sun and the fatigues of war.

Bk. VII.

Of meat the Mexicans did not make as much use as the Europeans; nevertheless, on the occasion of some banquet, and daily at the tables of the lords, several species of animals were prepared, such as deer, rabbits, Mexican wild boars, tuzas, Techichi, which they fattened, as is done among us with hogs, and other species of animals of the land, the water, and the air; but the most common were the turkeys, or Mexican peacocks, and the quails.

The fruits most in use among them were the mamei, the Tliltzapotl, the Cochitzapotl, the Chictzapotl, the pineapple, the chirimoja, the Ahuacatl, the anona, the pitahaja, the Capolin, or Mexican cherry, and various species of tunas, or Indian figs, which fruits made up advantageously for the lack of pears, apples, and peaches.

Amid so great an abundance of provisions the Mexicans were without milk and fat, since they had neither cows, nor sheep, nor goats, nor hogs. As for eggs, we do not know that any others were eaten by them, except those of turkeys, and of iguanas, whose flesh also they ate, and still eat.

The ordinary seasoning of their foods, besides salt, was chili pepper and the tomato, which have likewise become very common among the Spaniards of those countries.

They also used several kinds of wine, or drinks equivalent to it, of

§ 65. maguei, of palm, of maize stalks, and of the grain itself too, of which Wine. wine, called elsewhere Chicha, almost all the historians of America make mention: for it is the most general in that New World. The most usual among the Mexicans, and without doubt the best, was that of maguei, called Octli by them, and Pulque by the Spaniards (*). The manner of making it is

this

(*) Pulque is neither a Spanish word nor a Mexican one, but taken from the Araucanian language, which is spoken in Chile, in which pulcu is the general name of the drinks which those Indians use to get drunk; but it is difficult to guess how that name passed to Mexico.

p. 222

this. When the maguei, or Mexican aloe, reaches a certain size and Bk. VII.

maturity, they cut its stalk, or rather the still tender leaves from

which the stalk is formed, situated in the center of the plant, where a

sizable cavity remains. They scrape the inner surface of the thick

leaves that surround this cavity, and draw from it the sweet juice,

which distills from them in such abundance that a single plant is

wont to yield in six months more than six hundred pounds of juice,

and in the whole time of its fertility more than two thousand. (V)

They draw the juice from the cavity with a reed, or rather with a

long and narrow gourd, which serves them as a pump, and put it in a

vessel until it ferments, which happens in less than twenty-four

hours. To ease the fermentation, and to make the drink stronger, they

mix with it a certain herb, to which, however, they give the name of

Ocpatli, that is, remedy for wine. The color of this wine is white; the

taste somewhat harsh, and the strength sufficient to intoxicate,

though not so great as that of grape wine. For the rest, it is a very

wholesome drink, and valuable in many respects, since it is an excel‐

lent diuretic and an effective remedy against diarrhea. The consump‐

tion of this drink is astonishing, as is the profit which the Spaniards

derive from it. The duty collected on that alone which is consumed in

the capital amounts annually to almost three hundred thousand scudi,

only one Mexican real, or the eighth part of a scudo, being paid for

every twenty-five Castilian pounds. The quantity of Pulque which was

consumed in the capital in 1774 was 2,214,294½ arrobas, that is,

more than seventy-three million eight hundred thousand Roman

pounds,

not

(V) Vetancurt says that a maguei yields in six months twenty arrobas of pulque,

which are more than six hundred Italian pounds. He could well know it, having been

for many years parish priest of the Indians. Dr. Hernández affirms that from a single

plant as many as fifty amphoras are drawn. The Castilian amphora, smaller than the

Roman, contains, according to the calculation of Mariana, 512 ounces of wine or of

common water. Now, supposing that the Pulque weighs no more than water, the fifty

amphoras will be more than two thousand Roman pounds.

p. 223

not counting what is introduced by contraband, and what the privi‐

Bk. VII. leged Indians sell in the great square.

§ 66.

The Mexicans were not so singular in their dress as in their eating. Clothing. Their ordinary garb was very simple, being reduced altogether, for men, to the Maxtlatl and the Tilmatli, and for women to the Cueitl and the Huepilli. The Maxtlatl was a band, or broad girdle, with the two ends hanging in front and behind to cover the private parts. The Tilmatli was a square mantle, about four feet long; its two ends were knotted on the breast, or over one shoulder, as may be seen in our figures. The Cueitl, or Mexican skirt, was a piece of cloth, also square, with which the women wrapped themselves from the waist down to the middle of the leg. The Huepilli was a woman’s little shirt, or jacket without sleeves.

The clothing of the poor people was of maguei thread, or of mountain palm, or at best of coarse cotton cloth; but that of the well-to-do was of fine cotton adorned with various colors, and with various figures of animals, or of flowers, or even of cotton interwoven with beautiful feathers, or with fine rabbit fur, and decorated with several little gold figures, and with handsome tassels, especially on the girdle, or Maxtlatl. The men were accustomed to wear two or three mantles, and the women three or four little shirts and as many skirts, putting the longest ones underneath, so that some part of each might be seen. The lords dressed in winter in jackets of cotton interwoven with soft feathers, or with rabbit fur. The ladies wore, besides the huepilli, an overgarment somewhat like the surplice of our ecclesiastics, but broader, and with longer sleeves. (*)

Their shoes were nothing but soles of leather, or of coarse cloth of maguei, tied with laces in such a manner that they covered only the soles of the feet. The kings and the lords adorned the laces with rich ribbons of gold, and with gems.

All

(*) We have spoken elsewhere of the royal, the priestly, and the military dress.

p. 224

All the Mexicans wore their hair long, and held it a dishonor to be

Bk. VII.

shorn, except the virgins who were consecrated to the service of the

§ 67. temple. The women wore it loose, and the men tied it in various Their ornaments.

ways, and adorned the head with beautiful plumes, no less when they

danced than when they went to war.

It is difficult to find another nation that joined with such simplici‐

ty in dress so much vanity and luxury in the adornment of their bod‐

ies. Besides the feathers and the jewels with which they used to deco‐

rate their garments, they used ear-rings, pendants in the lower lip,

and many also in the nose, necklaces, bracelets, armlets, and even cer‐

tain rings in the manner of collars on the legs. The ear-rings and other

pendants of the poor people were of shells, of crystal, of amber, or of

some little glittering stone in the manner of a gem; but the rich wore

them of pearls, of emeralds, of amethysts, or of other gems set in

gold.

But their household goods did not correspond to such vanity.

§ 68. Household goods,

Their bed was nothing but one or two coarse mats of rushes, to

and domestic which the rich added fine mats of palm, and sheets of cotton, and the

occupations.

lords cloths woven of feathers. The pillow of the poor was a stone or

a piece of wood, and that of the well-to-do was perhaps of cotton.

The common people covered themselves in bed with nothing but the

tilmatli itself, or mantle; but the rich and the nobles used coverlets of

cotton and of feathers.

For dining, instead of a table they spread a mat on the ground.

They did indeed use tablecloths, dishes, bowls, pots, jugs, and other

such crockery of fine clay; but not, as it appears, spoons or forks.

Their seats were low stools of wood and of rush, or palm, or of a cer‐

tain species of reeds, which they called Icpalli. (*) In no house was the

Metlatl lacking, nor the Comalli. The metlatl was the stone on which

they ground the maize, and the

cacao

(*) The Spaniards, altering the name, call them Equipales.

Mexican dress.

p. 225

cacao, as it is represented in our figure of the way of making bread. This instrument is still in very common use throughout New Spain Bk. VII. and in the greater part of America. The Europeans too have adopted it, and in Italy and elsewhere the chocolate-makers use it to grind the cacao. The Comalli was, and still is (for it is as much used as the metlatl), a round, somewhat concave earthen dish, a finger in thickness and about fifteen inches in diameter.

The drinking vessels of the Mexicans were made of certain fruits resembling gourds, which grow in the hot countries on trees of moderate size. Some are large and perfectly round, and these they call Xicalli (X); the others are smaller and cylindrical, and to these they give the name of Tecomatl. Both kinds of fruit are solid and heavy: their rind is hard, woody, and of a dark green color, and their seeds are like those of gourds. The Xicalli is about eight inches in diameter, and the Tecomatl a little less in length and about four fingers in thickness. Each fruit, divided in half, yielded two equal vessels: they hollowed out all the seeds, and gave them, with certain mineral earths, a permanent varnish of a pleasant odor and of various colors, above all a beautiful red. Nowadays they are accustomed also to silver and gild them.

The Mexicans used in their houses neither candlesticks nor candles of wax or of tallow, nor did they use oil to give light; for although they made many kinds of oil, they employed them for nothing but medicine, painting, and varnishes, and although they extracted a great quantity

History of Mexico, Vol. II. Ff of

(X) The Spaniards of Mexico called the Xicalli Xicara. The Spaniards of Europe adopted this name to signify the little cup for taking chocolate, and from it arose the Italian chicchera. M. de Bomare mentions the tree of the Xicalli under the name of Calebasse d’Amérique, and says that in New Spain it is known by the names of Choyne, Cujete, and Hyguero; but this is not true. The name Hibuero (not Hyguero) was the one that the Indians of the island of Hispaniola gave to this tree: the conquering Spaniards formerly used it; but no one else afterwards made use of it in New Spain. The other names I have never heard in those countries.

p. 226

of wax from the honeycombs, they either did not wish to, or did not Bk. VII.

know how to, derive from it the advantage of light. In the maritime

countries they used for this purpose the cucui, or luminous beetles;

but in general they made use of torches, or brands, of Ocotl, which, al‐

though they gave a fine light and a good odor, smoked and blackened

the dwelling with soot. One of the European customs that they most

prized upon the arrival of the Spaniards was that of candles; but to

tell the truth, those peoples had little need of candles, since they de‐

voted to rest almost all the hours of the night, after having employed

all those of the day in labor or in affairs. The men worked at their arts

or trades, and the women at sewing, weaving, embroidering, making

bread, preparing food, and cleaning the house. All of them daily of‐

fered prayers to their gods, and burned copal in their honor, and

therefore there was no house, however poor, that did not have its lit‐

tle idols and its censers.

The manner that the Mexicans and the other American nations

had of getting fire was the same that the ancient shepherds of Europe

used (Y), that is, by the violent rubbing together of certain pieces of

wood. The Mexicans made use for the most part of achiote, which is

the roucou of the French. The Chevalier Boturini asserts that they got

it also from flint.

In the morning, after some hours of work, they took their break‐

fast, which was for the most part atolli, or polenta of maize, and their

dinner after midday; but among so many historians of Mexico I have

not found even one who makes mention of their supper. They ate lit‐

tle; but they drank often, now their wine of maguei or of maize, now

chia, now some drink of cacao, and now natural water.

After

(Y) Warm woods are the mulberry, the laurel, the ivy, and all those from which

fire-sticks are made. Scouts found this use in camps, and shepherds as well, since for

striking fire there is not always an occasion for a stone. Wood is therefore rubbed

against wood, and conceives fire by the friction, the fire being very easily caught by a

receiving material of dry tinder, fungus, or leaves. Pliny, Natural History, bk. 16, ch.

40. The same may be seen in bk. 2 of Seneca’s Natural Questions, and in other ancient

authors.

p. 227

After dinner the lords were accustomed to induce sleep with the smoke of tobacco. (Z) This plant was in great use among the Bk. VII. Mexicans. They used it in several plasters, and took it both in smoke § 69. by the mouth and in powder through the nose. For smoking, they put Use of tobacco. into a tube, or little channel, of wood, or of cane, or of some other more noble material, the leaf of the tobacco with the resin of the liquidambar and with other hot and fragrant herbs. They received the smoke by pressing the little channel with the mouth and stopping the nostrils with the fingers, so that it might more easily pass with the breath as far as the lung. Who would ever have believed that the use of tobacco, which the necessity of those phlegmatic nations invented, should be in the future a vice or a fashion general among almost all the peoples of the world; and that so humble a plant, of which the Europeans spoke and wrote so unfavorably, should become one of the greatest revenues of the kingdoms of Europe? But what ought to cause greater wonder, the use of tobacco being now so common among those very nations that censured it, is that it is yet so rare among its inventors, that there are very few today (I speak of the Indians of New Spain) who take it in smoke, and none who take it in powder.

Just as the Mexicans lacked the use of candles for giving themselves light, so too they lacked the use of soap for washing, although §Plants

used in place there were animals from which it could be got; (aa) but they made up of soap. well for such a lack with a fruit and with

Ff2 a

(Z) Tabaco is a name taken from the Haitian language. The Mexicans had two kinds of tobacco, very different in the size of the plant and of the leaves, in the shape of the flower, and in the color of the seed. The smaller, which is the common one, they called Picietl, and the larger Quaujetl. This grows as tall as a tree of moderate size. Its flower is not divided into five parts like that of the Picietl, but only distinguished into six or seven angles. These plants vary greatly by reason of the climate, not only in the quality of the tobacco but also in the size of the leaves and in other accidents; whence several authors have multiplied the species.

(aa) I have heard it said that from the Epatl, or zorriglio, an excellent soap is extracted.

p. 228

a root. The fruit was that of the Copalxocotl, a tree of moderate size, Bk. VII.

which grows in Michoacán, in Yucatán, in the Mixteca, and else‐

where. (bb) The pulp under the rind of the fruit, which is white, vis‐

cous, and very bitter, turns water white, foams, and serves like soap to

wash and clean linen. The root is that of the Amolli, a small plant,

very common in that country, to which the name of Saponaria ameri‐

cana is more justly suited, because it is less unlike the soapwort of the

old continent; but the Amolli is more used nowadays in washing the

body, and particularly the head, than clothes. (cc)

What we have set forth thus far concerning the political and eco‐

nomic government of the Mexicans is all that we have found worthy

of belief and of being brought to public light. Such were their public

and private customs, their government, their laws, and their arts,

when the Spaniards arrived in the country of Anahuac, whose war and

memorable events we wish to relate in the following books.
